Iranian currency plunges as new US ‘economic D-Day’ looms
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-24
16:14:42 UTC
01:14:42 KST

U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent is expected to announce new measures Monday evening aimed at further isolating Iran’s economy, in the hope that the sweeping pressure campaign he has described as the “largest economic offensive ever” will force the mullah regime to yield and compromise in stalled talks on ending the war.
Bessent is expected to detail the new measures at a special news conference. They will reportedly come on top of the naval blockade already being enforced against Iranian ports and include threats of sanctions against countries that continue to provide Iran with an economic “lifeline” and maintain business ties with it, which allow Tehran, among other things, to evade sanctions already in place. 
It remains unclear, however, whether President Donald Trump will follow through on such threats against Iran’s close allies, chief among them China, since sanctioning Beijing could risk reigniting a trade war that he has so far preferred to avoid. 
For now, it is not clear exactly what the new measures will include, but a source familiar with the matter told Reuters that they will expand the use of secondary sanctions the administration can impose on countries or other entities that violate restrictions on ties with Iran. The source described the move as a “final warning” to countries to sever their ties with the Islamic Republic, adding that failure to do so could result in their companies or other affiliated entities being cut off from the U.S. dollar-based financial system.
The source did not specify which new activities or sectors of Iran’s economy could be targeted, but said that in some Iranian sectors any activity, even in a third country, could also be subject to secondary sanctions. A senior administration official added that the Treasury Department had “mapped Iran’s oil smuggling and sanctions-evasion network,” and that the information would be presented to countries helping Iran circumvent sanctions as part of the warning issued to them.
Trump has shifted strategy in recent weeks following the deadlock in talks with Iran and Tehran’s refusal to allow the reopening of the Strait of Hormuz, the “oil artery” of the global economy, unless it receives U.S. recognition of its control over the strategic waterway. Rather than resume the war, Trump chose a policy of a suffocating economic blockade and last week promised to launch what he called an “economic D-Day,” invoking the Allied invasion of Normandy in World War II. He also threatened to punish countries that continue doing business with Iran.
So far, only the United Arab Emirates, which had been an important hub for Tehran’s efforts to evade sanctions, has formally announced that it is cutting all trade ties with Iran. But many analysts doubt whether economic pressure, even if additional countries join the effort, can force Iran into submission quickly.
Iran, which has already shifted to what has been described as a “survival economy” aimed at minimizing the effects of sanctions, has been dealing with them for decades. But its economic crisis has worsened significantly following the two most recent wars.
According to the International Monetary Fund, inflation in Iran is expected to approach 70% this year. Since the latest war, prices of some food products, such as meat, have risen by far more, as much as 150%, while the economy as a whole is expected to contract by a steep 5.4%.
Unemployment has surged, with at least 1 million people losing their jobs since the war broke out in late February. The Iranian currency, the rial, fell to a new record low of 1.9 million to the dollar at the beginning of the month and continued to slide ahead of the expected announcement of the new measures. On Sunday, it crossed the threshold of 2 million to the dollar, and on Monday it fell further to 2.02 million.
Against that backdrop, and just hours before Bessent’s news conference, Trump wrote in a brief post on Truth Social: “IRAN IS COMPLETELY COLLAPSING!!!”
New testimony is also emerging from frustrated Iranian residents who describe growing difficulty affording basic food products.
“We have basically given up on a lot of things,” 52-year-old taxi driver and father of two Ahmad Karimi told The Associated Press. Karimi said he works 15 hours a day to make ends meet. “We give up fruit, we give up protein, we have given up leisure time, and we even work on weekends.”
Karimi said he believes Iran’s leaders will eventually have to act soon to ease the hardship he and many others are experiencing.
“People can't keep living like this,” he said. “We’re just breathing, surviving and trying to get through one day at a time.”
Sadeq Mahmoudi, a 73-year-old Tehran resident, expressed pessimism that a solution could be found that would lead to the lifting of sanctions.
“There is no hope for an agreement and peace,” he told AP while waiting in line to buy U.S. dollars with the last of his savings.
Although senior officials in Tehran repeatedly say they are aware of the severe crisis and the hardship facing citizens, they insist that the Islamic Republic will not surrender and are now threatening renewed escalation, including attacks on countries that join the U.S.-led economic blockade.
Mohsen Rezaei, secretary of Iran’s Supreme National Security Council, warned Sunday: “If the economic war continues, not a single drop of oil will leave the Persian Gulf and the Strait of Hormuz. If Trump takes new aggressive steps, we will respond with force. If neighboring countries cooperate with the economic war, we will attack their interests. Any cooperation or support on their part will be interpreted as war."

Israeli Election Committee overrules AG, lets Ben-Gvir attend police confab
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-24
16:12:24 UTC
01:12:24 KST

Israel's Central Elections Committee chairman on Monday approved National Security Minister Itamar Ben-Gvir's participation in a police volunteers conference, overruling Attorney General Gali Baharav-Miara's position that he should not attend the event during the election campaign.
The chairman, Supreme Court Deputy President Noam Sohlberg, announced on Monday that he was rejecting out of hand the petitions against Ben-Gvir's participation, because Ben-Gvir pledged "not to deliver electioneering remarks at the event." Sohlberg explained that he preferred to reach a quick decision because the conference was scheduled to take place Monday evening. He advised Ben-Gvir to submit his remarks in advance for legal counsel's review.
Law enforcement sources told Haaretz on Sunday that Ben-Gvir and his ministry timed the conference to coincide with the election campaign. They added that Ben-Gvir planned to speak about his work before an audience of thousands, effectively engaging in illegal, police-funded electioneering. The petitions against Ben-Gvir's participation at the conference were filed by the Voters for Israel organization and the Zulat Institute.
Initially, the National Security Ministry planned to fund the conference, but due to legal issues, the police took over funding several weeks ago. The conference was set to take place in 2025, a year after the police volunteer program marked 50 years since its founding. In recent days, volunteers in the organization said that their commanders exerted heavy pressure on them to participate in the conference, where singers Moshe Peretz and Ivri Lider are expected to perform. However, due to low ticket purchases by volunteers, the police opened sales to rank-and-file police officers, as well as to relatives and friends of the volunteers.
The state stated in a filing submitted on Sunday that the tender for producing the event concluded only 10 days earlier. This statement contradicts Ben-Gvir's claim that the event's date was set before an election date was determined.
"According to the state's position, Ben-Gvir's participation in the event, including of course delivering remarks, is prohibited," the petitioners argued. "It raises serious concern that it would in practice constitute illegal electioneering and could even color the entire event with electioneering tones. His participation in the event amounts to the use of public resources for electioneering, contrary to the law."

Israel blocks 2 Swedish European parliamentarians from entering country
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-24
16:11:21 UTC
01:11:21 KST

Aysu Biçer
24 August 2026•Update: 24 August 2026
Two Swedish members of the European Parliament say they were prevented from travelling to Israel on Sunday, despite holding entry permits and supporting documentation.
Left-wing MEPs Jonas Sjostedt and Hanna Gedin were stopped as they were preparing to board a flight from Athens to Tel Aviv.
They had planned to travel on to the occupied West Bank and East Jerusalem, where they were due to meet representatives of the NGO Save the Children, visit refugee camps, and speak to Israeli and Palestinian human rights groups.
The two MEPs said they wanted to hear testimonies about what they described as deteriorating conditions and apartheid in the West Bank.
Sjostedt, of Sweden's Left Party, said they had been barred for political reasons because of their criticism of Israel and their calls for Israeli government ministers to be brought before the International Criminal Court.
“Israel is increasingly preventing people from entering the country, even members of parliament,” he said in a statement.
Gedin said it is unacceptable for Israel to prevent elected European officials from seeing conditions on the ground and meeting people affected by them while Israeli politicians continued to travel freely in Europe.
The Left group in the European Parliament said it had informed the EP President’s Office about the incident.
It described the refusal of entry as an attack on the institutions of the EU.
The incident comes as the Left group says ethnic cleansing in Palestine is continuing and amid concerns over negotiations by the European Commission on the sharing of biometric and personal data with Israeli security services.
Israel continues to block agreed quantities of food, medicine, medical supplies, shelter materials, and prefabricated homes from entering Gaza, where around 2.4 million Palestinians live, including 2.15 million displaced people, under catastrophic conditions.
Ongoing Israeli violations of the ceasefire have killed 1,286 Palestinians and injured 4,257 others as of Sunday, according to Gaza’s Health Ministry.
The ministry also said the overall toll from Israel’s war on Gaza since October 2023 had risen to 73,420 killed and 174,369 injured.

Malaysia to launch cloud seeding to combat haze as Indonesia battles forest fires
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-24
16:08:05 UTC
01:08:05 KST

Riyaz ul Khaliq
24 August 2026•Update: 24 August 2026
Malaysia is preparing to launch cloud seeding operations, including in Indonesian airspace, to combat worsening haze as neighboring Indonesia battles widespread forest and land fires, officials said Monday.
Malaysian Natural Resources and Environmental Sustainability Minister Arthur Joseph Kurup discussed the operations with his Indonesian counterpart Jumhur Hidayat, with Jakarta approving Malaysia's use of Indonesian airspace for the effort, according to Malaysian state-run Bernama News.
Cloud seeding involves dispersing substances into clouds to encourage rainfall, which can help reduce haze and assist efforts to contain fires. The two countries are discussing the timing of the operations along their shared border.
The move comes as the intensifying El Nino phenomenon and the Southwest Monsoon increase the risk of severe peatland and forest fires heading into October.
Fires have already affected around 285,000 hectares (704,000 acres) in Indonesia so far this year.
By Monday, 10 areas across four Malaysian states registered Air Pollutant Index readings in the unhealthy range, with the town of Serian approaching the "very unhealthy" threshold.
In neighboring Indonesia, around 3 million people have been exposed to smoke from forest and land fires, Health Minister Budi Gunadi Sadikin said, according to Antara News.
Berton Panjaitan, head of the communications center at Indonesia's National Disaster Management Agency (BNPB), told Anadolu that the country "is currently facing an elevated risk of forest and land fires, particularly in parts of Kalimantan and Sumatra."
“The situation is serious, but it remains under active control. Ground and aerial firefighting operations are being carried out simultaneously, including water bombing, aerial patrols and weather modification operations where conditions allow,” he said in a written statement.
He added that forest and land fires have been reported across several provinces since the beginning of the dry season, with "activity increasing significantly in recent months."
Local authorities have also been investigating 89 fire-related cases across nine provinces, Berton said.
Indonesia's president on Monday ordered the revocation of business permits held by corporations suspected of ordering forest and land fires.
Indonesian police have also named 72 suspects in forest and land fire cases across nine regional jurisdictions as authorities step up efforts to curb the blazes, broadcaster Kompas TV reported.
Police seized evidence including plastic bottles filled with diesel fuel in cases involving suspected deliberate arson, according to the report.

Bessent to speak as Trump administration expected to unveil new Iran sanctions
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-24
16:06:11 UTC
01:06:11 KST

Bessent to speak as Trump administration expected to unveil new Iran sanctions
Washington — Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent is holding a press conference Monday at the Treasury Department as the Trump administration announces the details of what it's calling "economic D-Day" for Iran.
Bessent is expected to broaden the administration's current sanctions regime with the threat of stronger secondary sanctions on entities and countries that transact with Tehran. The sanctions are part of the Trump administration's effort to apply economic tools to pressure Iran to reopen the Strait of Hormuz and eventually end the war that the administration originally said would last four to six weeks. That war is now in its sixth month.
After months of military operations and failed negotiations, Bessent plans to outline the "single greatest financial offensive ever marshaled against an adversary."
"President Trump has dismantled Iran's military capabilities, destroyed nearly 100 percent of its military factories, and buried its nuclear program," Bessent wrote in the Financial Times on Sunday. "We are now entering the endgame. At dawn begins an economic D-Day — the single greatest financial offensive ever marshaled against an adversary."
"The Islamic Republic has subsisted by dressing extortion as security guarantees," Bessent continued. "It has drawn strength from a calculus that regards Iranian retaliation as certain and American enforcement as negotiable. Under President Trump, that era is over. And those who fear the danger of defying Tehran ought not to discount the cost of testing Washington. The president has created the conditions to leverage every agency, every authority and action many assumed we would never summon. Our objective is to sever every economic lifeline that sustains the tyrannical regime until Tehran stands alone."
Mr. Trump has repeatedly declared Iran all but defunct, despite Iran's continued resistance to U.S. military efforts.
"IRAN IS COMPLETELY COLLAPSING!!!" Mr. Trump wrote Monday morning on Truth Social on Monday morning. The president has been insisting Iran has fallen for months.
This will be the latest round of Treasury action that targets Iran's economy. On Aug. 7, the department levied sanctions against a number of companies and individuals that it alleged laundered hundreds of millions of dollars. It was the eighth such action since the start of the second Trump administration.
"Every facilitator that keeps the regime afloat is putting a target on its own back," Bessent said in a statement at the time. "Treasury will continue to expose these networks and cut them off from the U.S. financial system."
Specifically, the department designated Shahr Bank and two Dubai-based exchange houses, Titan Exchange and Alps International, that it says helped Shahr Bank retrieve oil revenue for major Iranian exporters, including the National Iranian Oil Company and Naftiran Intertrade Co.
Several Iranian nationals were also designated, including Shahr Bank employee Saeed Ghasempour, who allegedly coordinated currency conversions with Russia's VTB Bank, and staff members of Iran-based Farab Soroush Afagh Qeshm, who allegedly managed invoices and payments for the scheme. A cluster of shell companies in Hong Kong, Singapore and Dubai — accused of funneling payments through intermediary accounts — was also designated.
Separately, the Treasury Department's Office of Foreign Assets Control sanctioned former Fly Baghdad CEO Basheer Abdulkadhim Alwan al-Shabbani for allegedly helping the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps-Qods Force move fighters, weapons, and money to militia groups in the region. Earlier this week, however, OFAC removed Fly Baghdad from its sanctions list.
The U.S. has been imposing sanctions on Iran for decades, although Tehran has often found ways to evade sanctions.
How to watch Bessent's press conference
- What: Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent speaks on Iran
- Date: Monday, Aug. 24, 2026
- Time: 1 p.m. ET
- Location: Treasury Building
- Online stream: Live on CBS News 24/7

A signal jammer found in Sofia’s Dragalevtsi district had been installed in an SUV and its owner has been identified, Prime Minister Rumen Radev said at a Council of Ministers press conference presenting the justice section of Bulgaria’s 2026–2030 government programme.
“Regardless of whether and how the device was discovered, and regardless of whether and how it interfered with air traffic and various electronic systems in Sofia, there is one indisputable fact: until recently, the police and security services protected these people; now their names are becoming known,” Radev said.
The Prime Minister urged people to call the 112 emergency number if they encounter convoys on the roads or conspicuous displays of private security at restaurants, saying the Interior Ministry was ready to respond.
“We will not allow the days of the mobsters to continue in Bulgaria. They have made a comeback in recent years,” Radev said, as quoted by BTA.

Ceasefire verification mission deploys to eastern DR Congo
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-24
16:04:37 UTC
01:04:37 KST

Ceasefire verification mission deploys to eastern DR Congo
A ceasefire verification mission comprised of representatives from DR Congo's government, the Rwanda-backed AFC/M23 rebel group, and observers from the International Conference on the Great Lakes Region (ICGLR) member nations arrived in South Kivu on Monday. The deployment came a day after the DRC and AFC/M23 agreed on a Switzerland-mediated roadmap for peace talks.
A mission to verify a fragile ceasefire between the Congolese army and Rwanda-backed AFC/M23 armed group was deployed Monday to the eastern Democratic Republic of Congo as the two sides traded accusations of abuses.
The eastern DRC has been riven by decades of conflict with multiple armed groups fighting over its mineral-rich territory, including the M23, which seized further swaths of the region last year.
Peace efforts led by Qatar and the United States last year sought to end the crisis, leading to the signing of two separate accords.
Watch moreUS cracks down on mineral trafficking in DR Congo with new sanctions
The Congolese government and the AFC/M23 signed a commitment towards a ceasefire in Doha in July 2025, while the DRC and Rwanda formalised a US-brokered peace deal in December in Washington.
However, fighting has continued on the ground.
On Sunday, the Swiss government announced in a statement that the DRC and AFC/M23 had agreed on a roadmap for peace talks, following negotiations held in Switzerland.
It also mentioned the upcoming deployment on the ground of a regional body to verify violations of the ceasefire to which the parties have committed.
Read moreDR Congo, AFC/M23 group agree on roadmap for peace negotiations after Swiss talks
The mechanism for verfiying ceasefire violations comprises Congolese government and AFC/M23 representatives as well as observers from member countries of the International Conference on the Great Lakes Region (ICGLR).
The mission, the first of its kind, was deployed on Monday by UN helicopters to Minembwe in South Kivu province, which has seen intense clashes in recent months, according to UN and government sources.
The mission will also travel to the lakeside town of Uvira, from where the AFC/M23 withdrew in January, as well as to the territory of Kalehe, where fighting has been reported in recent weeks, South Kivu governor Jean-Jacques Purusi told AFP.
Neither the DRC nor AFC/M23 authorities have yet reacted to the announcement of the mission.
Alleged abuses
Several international mediation efforts have failed to silence the guns in the eastern DRC, where a myriad of armed groups are active.
In April, the DRC and AFC/M23 agreed to facilitate humanitarian aid and release prisoners following talks in Switzerland.
An initial group of prisoners was handed over to the M23 by the Congolese government earlier this month.
New talks were held behind closed doors from August 17 to August 21 which included representatives from the United States, Qatar, Switzerland, the African Union Commission and Togo, serving as African Union mediator.
The two sides said they had "agreed on the importance of a standardised method for reporting allegations of ceasefire violations", according to the Swiss government statement on Sunday.
They also agreed to establish a "mechanism tasked with reviewing progress and addressing challenges in implementation of obligations and commitments under the Doha Framework Agreement", it added.
The DRC in a statement on Monday condemned alleged "crimes and serious human rights violations" committed by the M23 in the territories under its control and accused the group of killing "more than 300 people" in June and July.
Meanwhile, an AFC/M23 spokesperson on X accused government forces of having launched a "deadly attack" with artillery on a village near Minembwe.
Both sides have regularly accused the other of alleged abuses and ceasefire violations, often unverifiable by independent parties.
The area surrounding Minembwe, which has been fiercely contested for months, has been particularly difficult for international organisations to access.
Reports by NGOs and UN experts have said the AFC/M23 has also silenced dissenting voices, including journalists and human rights activists in areas under its control.
(FRANCE 24 with AFP)

Zelenskyy: Putin Talked Greatness for 27 Years—Now Russia is Queuing for Gas
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-24
16:04:26 UTC
01:04:26 KST

- Category
- War in Ukraine
Zelenskyy: Putin Talked Greatness for 27 Years—Now Russia is Queuing for Gas
Ukraine will not surrender its territory to Russia or ease the enormous military cost Moscow is paying for its invasion, President Volodymyr Zelenskyy said in a defiant Independence Day address on August 24.
Zelenskyy rejected the idea that handing over Donbas could quickly end Russia’s war of aggression against Ukraine, arguing that the Kremlin continues to misjudge both Ukraine’s willingness and its ability to resist.
We bring you stories from the ground. Your support keeps our team in the field.
“Putin is stuck in the past. He still looks at an old-style Ukraine—one that will stay silent, swallow it, endure it,” Zelenskyy said.
“That Ukraine exists nowhere anymore, except in his ‘little folders of reports.’ Our state has long been different. Not an observer, but a player. Not a victim, but a fighter.”
The Ukrainian leader said Kyiv would not allow Russia to regain the initiative or convince Ukraine’s partners that territorial concessions could provide a shortcut to peace.
“We will not let them seize the initiative, deceive Europe and the entire world into thinking that they simply need to give up Donbas, and everything will be over soon,” the President said.
Zelenskyy said there were no grounds for trusting such Russian promises and made clear that Ukraine would continue resisting rather than effectively rescuing Moscow from further battlefield losses.
“We have no illusions. We don’t trust them. We are not going to save the Kremlin half a million soldiers,” according to Zelenskyy.
He pointed to Russia’s repeatedly delayed ambitions to capture the entirety of Ukraine’s Donetsk region as evidence that Moscow’s military plans continue to fall short of its own timelines.
“Russia keeps pushing back the dates when they will ‘definitely, any day now, take the entire Donetsk region.’ It will keep pushing those dates back. Ukraine is different, and it does not give away what is its own,” he stated.
Zelenskyy also said Russia was preparing for another wave of mobilization rather than genuinely seeking an end to the war. For Ukraine, he said, the response must be to strengthen its military and ensure frontline troops have the weapons and equipment necessary to maintain the defense.
“We have to strengthen our military, have everything needed to hold our defense. Our warriors must be supplied and equipped with everything so that the replenishment of the enemy army can be turned into fertilizer for our soil.”
Zelenskyy ended his Independence Day address by directly challenging the expectations of Russian leader Vladimir Putin and contrasting Moscow’s original ambitions with the losses Russia has suffered during more than four years of full-scale war.
“Putin hopes that we are tired. But we know that he is mortal. He never understood us. Now he will not have time to. You wanted to take all our Ukraine, all our land; instead, you lost your oil refineries, your fleet, your profits, your entire generation that you sent into the meat grinder. You wanted us erased from the map; now you are absent from the international agenda of the future civilization,” he said.
Zelenskyy also pointed out the image of power the Kremlin has spent decades trying to project.
“For 27 years, the head of Russia has talked about greatness, while Russia is standing in lines for gasoline,” he concluded.
Earlier, reports emerged that fuel availability at Russian gas stations has plunged sharply, with gasoline reportedly available at only 28.1% of stations nationwide as authorities open dozens of antitrust cases against oil companies and independent fuel operators.

====================================================================================================

Netanyahu vows to raise Altalena wreck as political battle erupts over 1948 legacy
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-24
16:03:14 UTC
01:03:14 KST

Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu vowed Monday to raise the wreckage of the Altalena, the Irgun arms ship shelled during Israel’s 1948 War of Independence, after its remains were located off the Israeli coast nearly eight decades after it sank.
The announcement set off an immediate political battle over credit and memory surrounding one of the most divisive episodes in Israel’s early history.
Netanyahu delivered a separate statement before National Security Minister Itamar Ben-Gvir and Heritage Minister Amichai Eliyahu held their own news conference about the discovery. Ben-Gvir’s aides described Netanyahu’s intervention as an “embarrassing event,” while Ben-Gvir used his appearance to take a swipe at the prime minister, saying Menachem Begin, the Irgun commander who later became prime minister and a founder of Netanyahu's Likud party, “knew well the taste of persecution — the taste of people not wanting to be photographed with you on the same stage.”
Despite the clash over credit, both Netanyahu and Ben-Gvir used the occasion to warn against internal conflict.
Netanyahu said the Altalena “will be raised from the depths of the sea.”
He recalled then-prime minister David Ben-Gurion’s order to open fire on the ship in June 1948, weeks after the establishment of the state and the creation of the Israel Defense Forces.
“Ben-Gurion decided to open fire on the Altalena,” Netanyahu said. “It rained fire on our fighters during the War of Independence. Haganah forces on the shore opened a deadly barrage that claimed the lives of 16 Irgun fighters and three IDF soldiers.”
Netanyahu said he had attended annual Altalena memorial ceremonies for years and accused political rivals of desecrating them with what he described as incitement against his camp.
He also sought to claim a role in the long search for the ship.
“In 2009, I established the Heritage Department in the Prime Minister’s Office,” Netanyahu said. “A few years later, in 2019, I instructed that the search be renewed with a special budget. Using documentation from the Altalena captain’s logbook, today, finally, after 78 years, we found the ship after it sank into the depths.”
Ben-Gvir: ‘The lesson of Altalena is no civil war’
At his separate news conference, Ben-Gvir said the Altalena affair left a “bleeding wound” in Israeli history.
“They tried to make this wound disappear, to erase the legacy of the fighters who fought,” he said. “That is no coincidence. It is a symbol of the national camp, of its leader and later prime minister.”
Ben-Gvir also described the discovery as personally meaningful. His mother had been an Irgun member as a teenager, he said, distributing leaflets and being arrested.
“I grew up at home on those stories of heroism,” he said. “Every home in Israel, no matter the background or community, no matter who we vote for — there must never be a civil war. That is the lesson of the Altalena.”
Likud Minister Haim Katz, who joined Ben-Gvir’s news conference, credited researcher Roni Sadeh with persistently pursuing the ship’s location and the Heritage Ministry with providing dedicated funding for the search.
Political scramble for credit
The discovery quickly became entangled in election-season politics.
Ben-Gvir moved swiftly to claim credit, releasing a video with Eliyahu in which he declared: “Today is a historic day. We found the Altalena.”
“After so many years of searching, Heritage Minister Amichai Eliyahu and I are happy to announce — the Altalena has been found,” he said. “There is a message in finding the Altalena precisely now. Disagreements are allowed. Differences of opinion are allowed. But there must never be a civil war.”
Netanyahu and Defense Minister Israel Katz were initially left outside the announcement. Netanyahu’s office was reportedly surprised by the news of the discovery, despite having known about the search, while the Defense Ministry was apparently not informed.
Netanyahu later joined the public battle over the discovery, unsurprising given the Altalena’s deep association with Begin, whose Herut movement eventually became a central component of Likud.
A ship loaded with immigrants and arms
The Altalena sailed from France to the newly established State of Israel in June 1948 carrying about 930 immigrants, including Holocaust survivors, as well as a large shipment of weapons.
The ship belonged to the Irgun, the pre-state underground militia led by Begin. The crisis erupted just after the IDF was established and the separate Jewish underground organizations were being dismantled and integrated into a single national army.
Begin demanded that some of the weapons aboard the Altalena be allocated to Irgun units in Jerusalem. Ben-Gurion rejected the demand and insisted that all the weapons be handed over to the IDF.
When the dispute escalated, Ben-Gurion ordered military action against the vessel. In the ensuing fighting, 19 people were killed — 16 Irgun members and three IDF soldiers.
Begin, despite coming under fire, ordered his men not to retaliate in a way that could trigger a civil war, a decision that later became central to his political legacy.
Netanyahu promises to raise it — but experts question feasibility
The Altalena remained visible for months off the Tel Aviv coast before Ben-Gurion ordered it removed. Israeli forces later towed it farther out to sea and deliberately sank it.
The ship’s precise location was subsequently lost because records of the operation were incomplete or obscured.
Officials said Monday that the wreckage had been located about 20 kilometers, or 12 miles, off Israel’s coast at a depth of 503 meters, or about 1,650 feet, using sonar technology.
What happens next is less clear.
Netanyahu has promised that the Altalena will be raised, but recovering an entire vessel from that depth would be an extraordinarily difficult engineering operation and may prove impossible.
Among the alternatives being discussed are a floating memorial, a maritime heritage center or the recovery of selected parts of the wreck.
Veteran Yedioth Ahronoth journalist Shlomo Nakdimon, a former Begin adviser and author of a book on the Altalena affair, cast doubt on claims that the ship had been found intact.
“If they are talking about the Altalena as it once was — that does not exist,” Nakdimon said.
He said the lower part was sunk while the upper section had been dismantled and burned.
“Ben-Gvir and Eliyahu announced that they had discovered the Altalena in its entirety. It would take an act of God, because I saw it when it was sunk and it disappeared,” he said. “The lower section was sunk, the upper section was dismantled and set on fire. Irgun archives describe exactly where that section is located.”
The fight over the Altalena narrative
The discovery comes amid a broader political struggle over how the Altalena affair should be remembered.
About a year ago, seven ministers led by Eliyahu asked Defense Minister Israel Katz to remove the artillery piece known as the “holy cannon” — the IDF gun that fired at the Altalena — from the military’s Command and Staff College at Glilot and transfer it to the Menachem Begin Heritage Center in Jerusalem.
The cannon had been displayed at the senior officers’ college as a teaching tool about the tragedy and the importance of national unity.
The ministers argued that displaying it there sent a distorted educational message.
“The message is as though force and gunfire prevented disaster, while the truth is the opposite: restraint and the refusal to return fire saved the nation,” they wrote.
Katz later said he would order the cannon transferred so it could “express the true meaning of the event, out of deep appreciation for the work and legacy of Menachem Begin, who prevented a civil war.”

What is Portugal’s new ‘burqa law' banning face coverings?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-24
16:02:54 UTC
01:02:54 KST

Breadcrumb
What is Portugal’s new ‘burqa law’ and what does it mean for Muslim women?
A new law in Portugal banning face coverings in public spaces, believed to particularly target Muslim women who choose to wear the burqa, has sparked controversy and concerns from human rights groups.
The law, which was approved by parliament in July, came into effect after President Antonio Jose Seguro signed it earlier this month.
Local media in Portugal has dubbed the ban on face coverings as the ‘burqa law,’ as the bill originated with the right-wing Chega party.
A statement issued by the President’s office said: “The face must be considered a central element of human identity and communication”.
While the president noted that the issue was one of “great social and cultural sensitivity,” the decision has raised questions over how it will affect Muslim women who choose to wear the burqa, also known as niqab.
Here, The New Arab breaks down what the law says, and what it means for Muslim women.
What does the law state?
The law states that wearing any form of clothing which conceals or obstructs the face is prohibited.
Any violations of the law will incur fines of between150 Euros (£128) and 750 Euros (£641) if it is determined to be a case of negligence.
In cases where intent is proven, fines can range from 400 Euros (£342 GBP) to 3,000 Euros (£2,566).
Anyone found to be coercing another to cover their face on the grounds of religion, gender, origin or race can be jailed for up to three years.
The law clarifies that exemptions will be made for healthcare professionals and artistic, entertainment and advertisement reasons, and does not apply for places of worship, aircraft, diplomatic or consular premises.
How did it come about?
The law banning face coverings dates to October 2025, when parliament approved a first version of a draft bill that was pushed by the far-right Chega party.
The party, which has become a major political force, describes itself as a nationalist party with some of its members disparaging the Roma ethnic group and its founder, Andre Ventura, being accused of Islamophobia after he called for “the drastic reduction of the Islamic presence in the European Union”.
The party cited women’s rights and security concerns, noting it was for the “dignity and respect for women that should guide any state governed by the rule of law”.
Despite backlash from rights groups at the time, the bill on banning face coverings was passed on 17 July, with a two thirds majority in the Portuguese parliament.
Why is it controversial?
Activists and human rights commentators believe the law will unfairly target Muslim women and restrict freedom of expression.
Last month, Amnesty International issued a statement condemning the law.
“This new law is discriminatory and is a threat to human rights. Despite having removed direct references to Islam, which were a part of previous versions of the text, this new law clearly violates the human rights of Muslim women who choose to cover their faces,” the executive director of Amnesty's Portugal branch, Joao Godinho Martins, said.
Martins said the law was a “direct threat to their freedom of expression and freedom of religion,” and said that even though women’s rights were being cited as a justification for the law, the ban will likely have the opposite effect by excluding women who wear the burqa from public life and accessing education, employment and public services.
“The law also has implications for the right to privacy and the right to freedom of peaceful assembly, as it specifically prohibits the concealment of one’s face during demonstrations. This directly contradicts international law, which permits their use to ensure the possibility of participating anonymously and to prevent reprisals,” Martins continued.
Rights groups have called for the law to be reversed while some have noted a rising hostility towards Muslims in Portugal.
Which other countries have face covering bans?
The development comes after several countries in Europe have also adopted similar bans, while activists have raised concerns over polarisation in societies and noting it could pave the way for increased surveillance, unnecessary fines and conflict between communities.
France was one of the first countries to enact a full-face covering law in 2011, also arguing that face coverings prevent people from being identified and designating it as a potential security risk. Belgium also adopted a similar law in 2011, with anyone violating the law facing fines or up to seven days in prison.
Denmark, Austria and Bulgaria, too, have adopted laws against face coverings, restricting face-veils to places of worship and the homes.
Last year, Austria’s parliament approved a law banning the hijab for girls under the age of 14 in schools.
Some parts of Germany have bans in schools and public spaces on face-coverings, while Luxembourg bans them on public transport, schools and other common areas.

What to expect as Iran braces for new US economic measures amid war
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-24
16:02:50 UTC
01:02:50 KST

What to expect as Iran braces for new US economic measures amid war
Tehran may count on links with China and others to weather the storm, but average Iranian people will bear the brunt.
Tehran, Iran – Iranians are preparing for further economic pain as the United States gets ready to announce a new round of punishing sanctions on Iran.
US Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent is expected to announce new economic measures against the country on Monday, designed to “squash” the Iranian economy and “collapse” the government.
US President Donald Trump has also warned of an “economic D-Day”, with the US preparing the most potent financial measures against any country, aimed at Iran.
But analysts say the penalties are unlikely to change the behaviour of Iran, already one of the most sanctioned countries in the world.
Banks, oil buyers and refiners, shipping companies, registries, ports, airports, exchange houses, front companies and potentially governments facilitating Iranian trade could be exposed to secondary sanctions by Washington.
In a social media post on Monday, Trump claimed Iran was “completely collapsing”.
The country has been subject to a “maximum pressure” campaign by the US since Trump’s first term in office in 2018.
This followed the US’s unilateral withdrawal from a nuclear deal signed three years earlier between Iran and world powers.
This had lifted United Nations sanctions imposed on Iran in return for verified curbs on Iran’s nuclear programme.
Iran has responded to economic penalties by the US and its allies with a complex sanctions-evasion system. Among these are a shadow tanker fleet, ship-to-ship transfers of oil, shell companies, and alternative payment and barter mechanisms.
A naval blockade enforced by the US military on Tehran has physically stopped most Iran-linked ships from transiting through the country’s southern ports.
The US Central Command said on Sunday that its forces have redirected 70 commercial vessels, disabled three, and boarded two as part of the blockade, which it says could last indefinitely.
But Iran’s extensive land borders and railway lines, as well as access to the Caspian Sea in the north, gave Tehran more leeway to circumvent this.
Umud Shokri, energy strategist and senior visiting fellow at George Mason University, said that Iran has now become quite adept at finding ways around US sanctions.
“Iran has learned how to survive under pressure, although survival is not the same as avoiding economic damage,” he told Al Jazeera.
The Iranian rial continued to fall to a new all-time low on Monday, hitting a rate of 2.03 million rials against the US dollar in Tehran’s open market, as Iranians’ purchasing power weakens.
‘Language of force’
Senior military commanders in Iran remain defiant and have even indicated a more aggressive approach to deter the US from enacting more sanctions.
Mohsen Rezaei, the new secretary of Iran’s Supreme National Security Council, warned countries that if they back the new US economic measures, they will be considered enemies of Tehran.
“If the economic war continues, not a single drop of oil will be exported, neither through the Strait of Hormuz nor from anywhere in the Persian Gulf,” he said on Sunday.
Rezaei is considered part of the “old guard” of Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC), which has consolidated power around the office of new Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei.
Army chief Amir Hatami told a group of commanders during a speech on Monday that forces are prepared to “fight for 10-20 generations” if necessary.
“The only way is to make [the US] understand through a language of force that they cannot do what they intend” in Iran and the Strait of Hormuz, he said.
Iran’s Persian Gulf Strait Authority, established by military authorities to exert control over the Strait of Hormuz, has listed its own planned actions against ships in the vital waterway.
But President Masoud Pezeshkian said it is better to end the war with the US now in a position of strength.
Others have voiced concerns about the impact the conflict is having on Iran’s economic situation.
Another sign of Iran’s economic problems is the blackouts that regularly plague cities in the resource-rich country, and a range of planned austerity measures by the government.
Iran’s options facing the sanctions
In recent years, Iran has increased trade with China, Russia and other countries.
It has also relied on using local currencies, barter measures, intermediaries and discounts as a way around the sanctions.
Tehran has also expanded overland regional commerce via Iraq, Turkiye, Pakistan, the Caucasus and Central Asia. But the measures have not been able to make up for lran’s losses in oil income, hence the repeated domestic shocks.
Shokri said China is by far Tehran’s most important economic lifeline, due to the scale of its demand for Iranian oil. This has provided Tehran with a degree of protection, but even China has its limits.
“Beijing may oppose US sanctions politically, while major Chinese banks and globally exposed companies still avoid transactions that could threaten their access to the US financial system,” Shokri said. “Smaller refiners and less internationally exposed firms are usually more willing to take that risk.”
China’s Foreign Ministry said on Monday that sanctions and pressure will escalate tensions rather than solve the conflict.
Shokri said Central Asian countries and Azerbaijan can help Iran diversify transport and trade routes, but they lack the economic scale of China.
“So Iran’s strongest protection comes from China, while Turkiye, Iraq, the UAE, and other regional partners are more likely to reduce exposure if secondary sanctions become sufficiently credible and costly,” he said.

Nvidia begins production of Groq AI racks after $20B purchase
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-24
16:02:16 UTC
01:02:16 KST

Mücahithan Avcıoğlu
24 August 2026•Update: 24 August 2026
Nvidia said Monday that its Groq 3 LPX artificial intelligence racks have entered full production, marking the commercialization of technology acquired through the chipmaker’s record $20 billion purchase of Groq assets.
The systems will be deployed alongside Nvidia’s Vera central processing units and Rubin graphics processing units at cloud infrastructure provider Nebius and are expected to become operational later this year.
Nvidia bought assets from AI chip startup Groq in December in its largest-ever transaction.
The Groq 3 LPX is designed for low-latency inference, the process through which trained AI models generate responses. Faster inference is considered particularly important for AI agents and coding assistants, where delays can affect the user experience.
Each rack packages 256 Groq 3 chips and can generate about 3,400 tokens per second, according to a benchmark cited by Nvidia.
The Groq architecture places 500 megabytes of high-speed static random-access memory directly on each chip to reduce memory-related bottlenecks. The chips are manufactured by Samsung Electronics, while Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company produces Nvidia’s graphics processors.
Nvidia said the specialized systems are intended to complement rather than replace GPUs. While GPUs can perform both AI model training and inference, Groq chips primarily target the latency-sensitive “decode” phase of running models.
The company is also increasing shipments of its Vera Rubin systems, which entered production earlier this year.
Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang said in March that the company expects cumulative sales from its Blackwell and Vera Rubin platforms to reach $1 trillion through 2027. He also said a quarter of the data center capacity allocated to coding applications would use Groq chips.
Competition in specialized inference hardware has intensified as technology companies seek to make AI services faster and more economical. Nvidia rival Advanced Micro Devices has announced plans to integrate rack-scale systems with chips produced by Cerebras.

New research suggests women strategically use modesty to avoid backlash from female peers
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-24
16:00:49 UTC
01:00:49 KST

Women often navigate a difficult social landscape in the workplace, where promoting their own achievements can lead to negative social consequences. A new study shows that women anticipate this backlash and strategically use modesty to avoid being judged harshly by their peers. The findings were published in Evolutionary Psychological Science.
Women currently make up only a fraction of corporate leadership roles. One factor contributing to this stagnation is a reluctance to self-promote. Self-promotion involves highlighting personal achievements, abilities, and contributions to others. Because promotions often depend on visibility, individuals who actively self-promote are more likely to advance in their careers.
Women often encounter a backlash effect when they draw attention to their successes. Role congruity theory suggests that society holds specific expectations for how men and women should behave. Women are typically expected to be communal, cooperative, and modest. When a woman acts in an agentic or self-promoting manner, she violates these norms and often faces social penalties, such as being viewed as untrustworthy or unlikable.
These social penalties can be especially severe in interactions with other women. From an evolutionary perspective, human cognitive abilities evolved to help individuals navigate social networks. Historically, women often dispersed from their family groups and had to build alliances with non-relatives. Successfully managing these relationships required an understanding of social dynamics to secure coalition partners and resources.
Overt displays of superiority or ambition can threaten peers, sparking competition and risking the loss of these essential alliances. Researchers Krystal Duarte of Rutgers University, Jaimie Arona Krems of the University of California, Los Angeles, and Jennifer Byrd-Craven of Oklahoma State University designed an experiment to explore this dynamic. They wanted to know if women actively anticipate this backlash and adjust their behavior accordingly. The researchers focused on compliments, which serve as brief, everyday opportunities for self-promotion.
To test how people judge responses to compliments, the team recruited 838 undergraduate students from a midwestern university. The participants read a short scenario in which they imagined giving a compliment to a same-sex peer. The compliment focused on either the person’s appearance, expertise, general niceness, or job success.
Participants then reviewed four possible ways the peer might respond. Two responses were immodest: boasting, such as agreeing that they are the best, and neutral acceptance, such as a simple thank you. The other two were modest responses. One involved reflecting the praise back to the compliment-giver, while the other involved deflecting the praise by attributing success to luck.
After reading the responses, participants rated the fictional peer on traits like arrogance, warmth, and friendliness using a 100-point scale. They also rated how much they believed the peer liked them and wanted to be friends with them. The results showed that all participants judged boasting negatively. However, women judged boasting same-sex targets much more harshly than men did.
When the fictional target boasted, women rated the person as far more arrogant and less warm than men did. Women also reported feeling that the boasting target liked them less and would not want to initiate a friendship. Reflecting the compliment back to the giver resulted in the most favorable evaluations across all traits for both men and women. Neutral acceptance fell in the middle, eliciting less negative reactions than boasting but fewer positive reactions than reflecting.
Deflecting the compliment, which the researchers initially categorized as a modest response, resulted in surprisingly negative evaluations. The team suspects that deflecting praise might be interpreted as rejecting the compliment-giver’s friendly intent. By failing to accept the compliment, the recipient may appear dismissive or unwilling to connect.
In a second experiment, the researchers investigated whether people anticipate these social judgments and change their behavior as a result. They recruited 505 undergraduate students who imagined receiving a compliment from a same-sex peer. The compliments in this experiment were restricted to either physical appearance or job success, representing two highly visible markers of status.
Participants indicated which of the four response types they would most likely use in that situation. They also rated how they expected the compliment-giver would judge them based on their chosen response. The goal was to see if women actively try to avoid the penalties observed in the first study. The data revealed a gender difference in preferred responses.
Women were much more likely than men to choose modest responses, particularly reflecting the compliment back to the giver. Men were more likely to select neutral acceptance or boasting than women were. When participants imagined how the other person would view them, women anticipated severe social penalties for immodest responses. Compared to men, women strongly expected that boasting would lead the other person to view them as arrogant, cold, and unlikable.
Women also expected that the compliment-giver would assume they had no desire to be friends. These anticipated penalties were sensitive to the context of the compliment. Women expected the harshest judgments when boasting about their physical appearance. The researchers note that physical appearance is closely tied to mate value and social comparison, making it a sensitive area for peer interactions.
The experiments utilized hypothetical written scenarios rather than live interactions. In actual face-to-face conversations, nonverbal cues like tone of voice, facial expressions, and body language heavily influence how a statement is received. A response that reads as boastful on paper might be interpreted as a joke if delivered with a smile. The study was also limited to same-sex interactions.
The social rules governing modesty and self-promotion often change in mixed-sex environments. Future research would need to examine whether women evaluate men differently than they evaluate other women in similar scenarios. Additionally, the participants were young adults living in the United States. Cultural norms surrounding modesty and self-enhancement vary widely around the world.
In many East Asian cultures, social norms place a much stronger emphasis on downplaying personal achievements compared to Western cultures. The negative interpretation of deflecting a compliment might be unique to a Western context. Despite these limitations, the research indicates that modesty is more than just a polite habit.
For many women, modesty functions as a strategic tool to manage relationships and avoid alienating peers. As modern workplaces increasingly reward assertiveness, women are forced to navigate a challenging line between advancing their careers and maintaining their social networks.
The study, “Strategic Modesty in Women’s Same-Gender Interactions: Response to Compliments and Anticipated Backlash,” was authored by Krystal Duarte, Jaimie Arona Krems, and Jennifer Byrd-Craven.

How Israel’s efforts to expel UNRWA from Jerusalem and crack down on West Bank refugee camps are connected
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-24
16:00:00 UTC
01:00:00 KST

The UN Relief and Works Agency (UNRWA), which is in charge of Palestinian refugee camps in Palestine and the region, is bracing for an “imminent” Israeli takeover of its premises in Jerusalem, according to a statement by the agency’s ًWest Bank affairs director, Roland Friedrich, on August 14.
In the days prior to the statement, Israeli municipal authorities in Jerusalem entered the agency’s premises in the Shufaat refugee camp north of Jerusalem, which includes UNRWA’s schools complex, health center, and the camp’s community services office, the statement said. Private contractors began work in the buildings as well.
Israeli media quoted Israel’s Jerusalem municipality as saying that the properties do not belong to UNRWA but are privately owned, with which the municipality has signed use agreements. UNRWA, however, says the Israeli push to take over its premises in Shufaat is running simultaneously to similar moves on the agency’s vocational training center in the Qalandia refugee camp, also north of Jerusalem.
The moves in Shufaat and Qalandia are the latest phase of a long Israeli campaign to remove UNRWA’s presence from the city, and with it, the legal recognition of a refugee population Israel does not want acknowledged within the city’s limits.
Why the north of Jerusalem?
Both Qalandia and Shufaat were established after the Nakba of 1948 to house Palestinians displaced from their homes in what became Israel proper. They are located in the northern periphery of Jerusalem, under the jurisdiction of Jerusalem’s municipality, and under Israel’s control since its occupation of eastern Jerusalem in 1967.
However, Israel has physically isolated Qalandia and Shufaat from the city with the separation wall, which it began building in the early 2000s, marginalizing residents by diminishing municipal services to the two areas, while at the same time preventing the Palestinian Authority from exercising any presence within them. This has effectively turned Qalandia and Shufaat into administrative grey zones and no-man’s-lands — sitting between the Israeli annexation wall and Ramallah, and plagued by overcrowding, poverty, and crime.
In these conditions, UNRWA is one of the few institutions providing services to the Palestinian refugee population, particularly education through its schools and vocational centers.
“The schools and the vocational center are very important to the population, because they provide free education to families who have limited income, and limited access to the Palestinian economy in Ramallah,” a resident of Qalandia who asked not to be named told Mondoweiss. “Without UNRWA schools, most young people in Qalandia and Shufaat and other refugee camps wouldn’t have a chance at an education, and without the vocational center in Qalandia, many of them wouldn’t have a chance to learn a profession, because most families can’t afford college fees.”
For Jamal Jumaa from the Palestinian grassroots Stop The Wall campaign (STW), the isolation of the Palestinian population in the north Jerusalem camps is one part of Israel’s strategy to eliminate the refugee question. “Shufaat, Qalandia, and the surrounding areas that are part of Jerusalem’s municipal limits are home to more than 120,000 Palestinians, whom Israel has effectively isolated from the city’s interior,” Jumaa told Mondoweiss. “Many Palestinians whose homes are demolished in Jerusalem, or those who are Jerusalem residents and marry West Bank Palestinians, end up living in this grey zone.”
In this sense, Shufaat and Qalandia are similar to other neighboring grey areas, serving as effective dumping grounds for Israel’s “unwanted” Palestinian Jerusalem residents. But where Qalandia and Shufaat are unique, Jumaa says, is in their status as refugee camps, and Israel doesn’t want to have a refugee population residing in Jerusalem’s outer limits.
“This is why Israel is targeting UNRWA’s institutions in these camps,” Jumaa explained. “UNRWA’s presence is the physical representation of the refugee status of the population.”
Removing the agency from the area, therefore, would erase the refugee status of Palestinians in those areas.
Why UNRWA?
The UN agency was created by a UN resolution in December 1949, seven months after Israel was admitted to the UN as a member state. Israel’s admission into the international organization was conditioned by its implementation of UN Resolution 194, which stipulates Palestinian refugees’ right to return and compensation — a condition which Israel accepted by signature, but never implemented. UNRWA received the mandate to provide humanitarian services to Palestinian refugees, establish refugee camps, and maintain the registry of Palestinian refugees and their descendants as holders of the right of return.
Israel has been pushing to abolish UNRWA for years, arguing that the agency perpetuates the Palestinian refugee problem. For Palestinians, UNRWA is the representation of the international recognition of the Palestinian refugees’ plight and an institutional witness to the Nakba. More crucially, it is the international entity responsible for providing education, health, employment, and legal status to Palestinian refugees deprived of these rights as a result of the absence of a Palestinian state. From both perspectives, UNRWA is central to the Palestinian cause.
In October 2024, Israel passed a law banning UNRWA’s work in Israel. Then in December 2025, Israeli forces took over the agency’s headquarters in the Sheikh Jarrah neighborhood in east Jerusalem, after a series of attacks by Israeli individuals on the surroundings of the headquarters.
Israel’s crackdown on UNRWA followed months of financial strangulation of the agency led by the U.S., which halted its share of funding for it in January 2024 following Israeli allegations that 12 UNRWA employees in Gaza had taken part in the October 7 attacks.
The UN Office of Internal Oversight Services (OIOS) investigated 19 staff members and, in August 2024, concluded that nine employees “may have been involved” in the attacks, resulting in their termination.
The investigation found insufficient evidence to support allegations against nine other staff members, while no evidence was obtained for one additional case, which was closed. UN officials noted that the OIOS could not independently authenticate most of the information provided by Israeli authorities.
What comes next?
The current escalation in Qalandia and Shufaat follows a pattern of intensifying Israeli pressure on the two camps.
In February, Israel raided Qalandia and demolished multiple businesses and structures adjacent to the main street; in May, Israeli forces raided Qalandia again and threatened residents with displacement; and earlier this month, Israeli forces announced a “large-scale operation” in the camp that lasted several days, during which they arrested dozens of Palestinians, interrogated them in the field, and released all but one. In all these raids, residents feared that the UNRWA premises would be targeted, which the Qalandia resident affirmed.
“Every time, residents brace for the takeover of the vocational center for the municipality,” the resident said. “Every time, Israeli forces threaten residents with arrest if they oppose their actions in the future, which confirms that they might be planning something to come, possibly against UNRWA.”
For residents, the stakes would go beyond buildings. “It would be more than the loss of a center that formed countless skilled workers, or the schools, or the health center,” the resident said. “It would be the beginning of imposing Israel’s direct control inside the camp, erasing its identity. If Israel succeeds in that, it could be the beginning of doing the same in other refugee camps.”

Dependence Day: A generation after the Soviet Union fell, Ukraine is a ruined proxy of the West
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-24
15:56:49 UTC
00:56:49 KST

Dependence Day: A generation after the Soviet Union fell, Ukraine is a ruined proxy of the West
There is one feeling I often recall from having lived and worked in Ukraine – to be precise, in its most nationalist, full-of-itself city, Lviv – for a total of five years in the 2000s: Thank God, at least there’s no violence!
That is what I, some other foreigners, and quite a few Ukrainians, too, were thinking and even saying then. The country was politically and even culturally polarized, extremely corrupt, and a sham ‘democracy’ even more fake than most of the rest.
It was dominated and bled dry by a greedy, hypocritical, and – despite all the cheap talk, the pious singing-of-anthems-hand-on-heart, and the cheesy peasant braids and stitched shirts – deeply unpatriotic establishment of typically post-Soviet turncoats, thieves, liars, and sell-outs. There was no rule of law, the courts were a bad joke, and the police was a racket.
And yet, while the usual business-crime complex took its toll and there was the occasional state murder of a journalist, there was no large-scale violence. There was bitter grumbling galore, including about Crimea, all the time. But there was no civil war – as the almighty US intelligence services had infamously as good as predicted in the even darker 1990s – and no one was reaching for a gun either to leave an unbearable country or keep others in it by force.
While the Americans were busy thinking about the possibility – and perhaps opportunities – of Ukrainians killing each other in large numbers, their British cousins were considering another kind of doom. For them, more than three decades ago, the most interesting question was whether Ukraine and Russia would go to war, specifically over Crimea. There, British spooks back then warned with unusual honesty, “most of the population is Russian,” and things might become less than cordial if Kiev didn’t watch its steps.
And here we are now: On the 35th official anniversary of its existence outside the former Soviet Union, Ukraine has become the sum of all fears, much worse than even its pervasively corrupt, brutally unfair, and de facto lawless pre-2014 version. It is now ruled by its worst, most corrupt, and most authoritarian and bloody regime ever – hands down – under Vladimir Zelensky and his cronies. They steal, embezzle, and launder piles of dirty money as if there’s no tomorrow, which, to be fair, there really probably isn’t for them, at least not in Ukraine.
They literally hunt and, in effect, sell their subjects as cannon fodder, so that they can profit and the West can have its slaughterhouse war against Russia, in an insane, evil, and futile attempt to eliminate Moscow as a power to be reckoned with. This is the reality of “busification,” and nothing remotely like it happened before Zelensky and his accomplices took over. Those who do not readily submit to being treated as combat cattle regularly die mysteriously from ‘falls’ or maybe just blowing their nose.
The Ukrainian mass media have never been remotely clean, objective, or reliable. But the zombified and self-zombifying propaganda machine created under Zelensky is unprecedentedly awful. Those who openly resist the national brainwashing are incarcerated by kangaroo courts, tortured, and killed.
Or consider basic demography: Ukraine’s real population has almost halved since the end of the Soviet Union, and the future looks even worse. And while part of this catastrophe is due to the current war, much of it has been produced by the ceaseless class-war-from-above of injustice, exploitation, and hopelessness that Ukraine’s ‘elites’ have waged on their own subjects for a third of a century while blabbing sanctimoniously about nation this and nation that.
And the results of all these horrors? They are abysmal even on their own perverse terms. Just look at the map: Crimea is gone and so are substantial portions of Ukraine’s post-Soviet east due to a combination of regional rebellion, Kiev’s cack-handed, violent attempts to stomp it out, and a war with Russia. All of this could have been avoided.
Yet it was not. Indeed, Ukraine’s short post-Soviet history is, among other sad things, a record of at least sleepwalking and, more frequently, proactively provoking this twin catastrophe. But Ukrainians – or rather their rotten establishment – did have plenty of help in getting it so wrong. Take, for instance, the mean-spirited and asinine obsession with suppressing the Russian language to eradicate Ukraine’s windfall advantages as a mostly bilingual country of Russian and Ukrainian speakers.
That perverse and self-harming pursuit – recalling Freud’s hunch about nationalism as neurosis – was obstinately cultivated by both a narrow-minded but hyperactive and arrogant minority of ukrainier-than-though nationalists at home and descendants of run-away World War II nationalists, mass murderers of Poles, and Nazi collaborators from the West who were eager to infest post-Soviet Ukraine with their hoary bigotry.
Personally, I remember being naïve enough back then in the 2000s to ask Lviv acquaintances why Ukraine couldn’t just codify its own variant of Russian (comparable to the way American English is different from British English) and also promote Ukrainian (I know both languages, as it happens), and then focus on actually important matters. Blank stares were the answer, at best.
Ironically, well into the current war, there was a short news blip about someone in Ukraine having discovered the same obvious idea. How catastrophe can concentrate the mind and how sad that even now, after an estimated 1.5 million battlefield casualties, such pragmatic thinking is still a minority taste: Kiev’s policy is simply to double down on a daft campaign to win the lexicon while losing the war.
Entitled, patronizing exile nationalists projecting their selfish tunnel-vision fantasies on, and throwing their Western hard currency at, desperate people freshly shell-shocked by ultra-predatory ‘transition’ capitalism were one noxious outside influence vitiating Ukraine’s post-Soviet trajectory. But it was far from the only one. Indeed, as time went on, the West as a whole – its politicians, intellectuals, and mainstream media – became ever more interested in and vastly detrimental to Ukraine.
To be fair, the first yet already extremely harmful steps the West took to, ultimately, ruin Ukraine had very little to do with it: When Washington decided – not once but repeatedly – to cheat and humiliate Russia and break clear promises not to expand NATO eastward, it was first Germany, then multiple Eastern European countries that were at the center of attention, but not Ukraine. Yet, ironically, even while neglecting it, the West’s reckless expansion already set the stage for phase two of putting Ukraine in the crosshairs.
That was reached in 2008, when, at an infamous summit in Bucharest, the high and mighty of the West, led by the indispensably destructive US, artfully maneuvered Ukraine into the very worst position imaginable: by making a promise of NATO membership for Ukraine sometime in the future, they inevitably poked Russia in the eye once too often. By keeping the reckless promise vague, they left Ukraine completely exposed, as if baiting Moscow to intervene. The whole mad scheme took willfully ignoring the clear warnings issued by Russian President Vladimir Putin in Munich one year before and multiple equally explicit warnings produced by not Russians but widely acknowledged Westerners such as George Kennan and, back then, even William ‘Bill’ Burns.
Georgia shared this fate – lethal promises, no back-up – with Ukraine. And yet even after a short war there quickly demonstrated that this time Russia would stand its ground and defend its basic national security interests, the West stayed its course in Ukraine.
Indeed, it doubled down on it. If only the West had dropped Ukraine the way it dropped Georgia in 2008, many Ukrainians would still be alive and the country would have lost none or far less territory. Instead, the West got involved ever more deeply, offering a poisonous EU association agreement and the shaping a regime-change coup in 2014. After that, it bound Ukraine so closely into official and black-ops NATO structures that formal membership looked increasingly irrelevant, while helping Kiev sabotage the last realistic chance to return to peace and avoid large-scale war, the Minsk II agreement of 2015. Meanwhile, demagogic public intellectuals, such as Tim Snyder and Anne Applebaum did their worst to pour oil into the fire by a mix of silly nationalism-by-proxy and nasty Russophobia.
Finally, the West almost nonchalantly refused to seriously consider Moscow’s, as it turned out, last pre-February-2022 offer to return to substantial diplomacy and compromise. Once the war had greatly escalated, the West sent Britain’s most evil clown to kill an excellent opportunity to make peace quickly produced by the Istanbul talks of spring 2022.
Since then, since locking Ukraine into a devastating, perfectly unnecessary, and futile yet deliberately provoked war to weaken Russia, things have only gotten worse and worse, and there seems no end in sight. Indeed, as a remarkably sane former German general has just explained, Kiev is still eager to drag all of Western Europe (at least) into an open shooting war with Russia. In Europe, many establishment figures seem all too willing to go along. Ukraine has become the outstanding example of how the West risked and then destroyed the opportunities of the post-Cold War period. Ukraine could have been a prosperous country profiting from links to both East and West. Instead it’s a ruin.
